Finally, we experimentally test how people flexibly shift to proximal social learning when conditions favour it.

Model

We model social learning in domains where traits are organized hierarchically. We consider traits in networks of prerequisite relationships, so some traits must be acquired before others become learnable. Recent models indicate that relationships among traits influence cultural change 7, [81] [82] [83] [84] , but focus on population-level cultural dynamics rather than constraints on trait acquisition. Our model instead focuses on how dependencies in trait hierarchies constrain what individuals can learn at a given stage, and how these constraints alter the effectiveness of social learning strategies.

Trait hierarchies are represented as acyclic networks in which no trait depends directly or indirectly on itself. Networks have a single "root" representing a dummy trait that anchors the hierarchy. Individuals build their repertoires incrementally over time. For example, a hunter learning to track animals must first learn to recognize species from their footprints and interpret the signs along the trail before they can infer where the animal is likely heading 85.

Trait acquisition occurs through social learning. Learners observe demonstrators and attempt to acquire traits that they do not yet possess. These demonstrators are part of a population that is stable and identical across learning strategies, allowing us to isolate how different strategies cope with hierarchical learning constraints rather than differences in population dynamics.

We consider four fundamental social learning strategies ("biases" 4, 8 ) that determine the probability that a trait is targeted for learning: (i) payoff bias, a preference for targeting traits associated with high payoffs 2, 4 ; (ii) prestige bias, a preference to copy demonstrators with high total payoffs 43, 45 ; (iii) conformist bias, a preference for targeting common traits 4, 27 ; (iv) proximal bias, a preference to copy demonstrators with few traits not in one's current repertoire 53, 74 . As a benchmark, we consider random (unbiased) social learning.

We evaluate the effectiveness of each strategy by the payoff individuals accumulate as they build their repertoires over time. To do so, we start with several simplifying assumptions that we relax later. In each time step, a learner attempts to acquire a single trait. If they possess the required prerequisites, the trait is acquired; otherwise, the learning attempt fails. Learners do not know the prerequisite relations between traits and therefore cannot directly determine which traits are within reach. Each trait contributes positively to performance, but trait payoffs are independent of position in the hierarchy. Basic traits tend to be more common than advanced ones, because they can appear in more repertoires (for example, most people can add and subtract, but few master linear algebra).

To examine how trait hierarchies affect social learning strategies, we vary the structure of the trait networks, changing how strongly learning is constrained by prior acquisition. Traits may have multiple prerequisites (e.g., hunting large game requires both tracking and archery skills), and a single trait may serve as a prerequisite for multiple other traits (e.g., controlling fire unlocks both roasting meat and smoking food for preservation; see Fig. 1 for example networks). For each network, we quantify the degree of constraint by a normalized measure R ranging from 0 (traits can be learned independently) to 1 (maximally constrained learning), which captures how strongly trait dependencies restrict which traits are learnable at a given stage (Supplementary Table 1). For networks up to eight traits, we exhaustively analyse all possible trait hierarchies and payoff assignments, allowing us to characterize how learning constraints systematically shape the performance of social learning strategies. Model details are provided in the Methods.

Results

Trait hierarchy fundamentally reshapes which social learning strategies are effective (Fig. 1). When traits can be independently learned (R=0), payoff bias dominates, confirming standard model predictions 4, 19, 30, 36, 47 because learners can directly copy high-payoff traits. However, once trait hierarchy constrains learning, payoff bias breaks down: learners target high-payoff traits for which they lack the prerequisites. Prestige bias fails for a similar reason, as highprestige individuals often exhibit advanced traits beyond the learner's reach. 1 for a larger set); nodes and edges represent traits and their prerequisites, with colour intensity indicating relative payoffs. The white top node represents the root (naive state). For details and robustness tests, see Supplementary Figs. 2 3 4 5 6.

In contrast, trait hierarchy strongly favours conformity and, especially, proximal social learning (Fig. 1). Both strategies systematically avoid unlearnable targets. Conformity exploits the fact that basic traits are more prevalent, biasing learners towards learnable traits. Proximal social learning goes further, directing learners to demonstrators who express traits within their "zone of proximal development", enabling stepwise progression through hierarchical sequences of traits. Importantly, neither strategy requires knowledge of dependencies between traits as they succeed by leveraging observable cues that implicitly track learnability: trait frequency (conformity) and repertoire similarity (proximal social learning).

The model analysis so far considers a minimal case that isolates the core consequences of hierarchical learning constraints. Real-world learning environments, however, vary in the size of skill domains, the observability of traits, the rigidity of prerequisite relationships, and learners' understanding of the underlying hierarchy. Exploring these key conditions shows that proximal social learning is favoured across a wide range of settings (Fig. 2).

For larger learning domains (Fig. 2A-C), an increasing share of traits lies beyond novices' reach, causing payoff and prestige bias to systematically misfire by targeting unlearnable traits. Conformity performs better, but loses traction as the number of traits increases: even the most common traits become too rare for frequency to reliably signal learnability.

When learners are more likely to observe advanced or high-payoff traits (e.g., when demonstrators prefer to express those), trait hierarchy tends to expose learners to fewer learnable traits. This increases the likelihood of learning failures for payoff and prestige bias, reducing the performance of these strategies (Fig. 2D-F). In contrast, proximal social learning remains effective across these scenarios because it consistently directs learners toward learnable traits, avoiding the error of targeting traits that cannot yet be learned.

The results are robust to relaxing the assumption that all prerequisites are strictly required for learning (Fig. 2G-I). In real-world learning domains, some foundational traits may primarily improve the execution of downstream skills, or may be partly substitutable, allowing learners to occasionally acquire advanced traits without fully mastering all prerequisites. Consistent with this, softening dependencies partially restores payoff bias (Fig. 2H), because learners can occasionally acquire traits that would otherwise be beyond their reach. However, the core problem remains: as long as dependencies continue to constrain what learners can reliably acquire, payoff-biased learners still often target unattainable traits. Under these conditions, repertoire similarity and especially trait frequency remain reliable cues for learnability, allowing proximal social learning and conformity to prevail.

Payoff bias can recover when learners have substantial knowledge of the prerequisite relationships between traits (Fig. 2J-L). In real-world learning environments, individuals may sometimes recognize which traits are beyond their current capabilities and avoid attempting to learn them. To capture this, we allow learners to discount traits according to how many prerequisite traits they lack, increasing the transparency of the hierarchy and thereby improving the performance of payoff bias by filtering out unattainable targets (Fig. 2K). However, this advantage emerges only when learners have near-complete knowledge of the trait hierarchy (Fig. 2L). Unless individuals can reliably identify which traits are within reach, payoff bias continues to misfire, undermining its performance when learning is constrained.

Hierarchical skill structures favour proximal social learning in real-world domains

The preceding analyses systematically characterize how trait hierarchy shapes the performance of social learning strategies under controlled conditions. We next examine how these findings translate to plausible skill hierarchies from four real-world domains: navigation, tracking, telemarketing, and mathematics (Fig. 3). Using ethnographic 85, 86 , educational 87 , and labour market data 69 , we constructed networks representing potential prerequisite relationships between skills (Methods). These domains span diverse activities and vary in the strength of trait hierarchy, allowing us to assess how such structures shape strategy effectiveness (abstracting from institutional or social structures that may partially sequence learning and constrain learners' choice of demonstrators). The difference between telemarketing and mathematics, despite similar levels of constraint, is consistent with the stronger effects observed in larger domains (Fig. 2A-C).

The inferred networks vary in size and hierarchical structure. Applying our model to these networks reproduces our core result: as hierarchy strengthens, strategies that target unlearnable traits falter (Fig. 3E-H). Accordingly, payoff bias performs well only in the relatively flat navigation domain, but breaks down in more hierarchical settings. In contrast, proximal social learning consistently performs best in tracking and telemarketing, with conformity as a secondary strategy. These applications illustrate how hierarchical dependencies in real-world skills can shape social learning and point to a testable prediction of our model: in domains with strong prerequisite constraints, learning should be guided less by payoff and more by proximity in knowledge or skills.

Experience tunes social learning to constraints

The analyses above assume fixed strategies, consistent with mainstream cultural evolution literature. However, real learners encounter environments that differ in their constraints, and recent work increasingly emphasizes that people adapt their social learning strategies through experience 40, [88] [89] [90] [91] [92] . To capture this and generate predictions about how learning environments shape social learning over time, we simulate learners who update their strategy use through reinforcement learning. Each time step, individuals choose a strategy based on its expected payoff and revise this expectation after observing outcomes (Methods). We focus on payoff bias and proximal social learning, and consider domains with either no constraints (R=0) or maximal constraints (R=1). Simulations vary the proportion p of encountered domains with maximal constraints (proportion 1 -p of domains are unconstrained). Learners begin by using both strategies with equal probability, but adjust their strategy use as they accumulate experience across environments.

Strategy use quickly tracks learning constraints (Fig. 4A). When learning is unconstrained, learners quickly converge on payoff bias; when constraints are strong, they shift toward proximal social learning. As the frequency of constrained domains (p) increases, reliance on proximal social learning rises smoothly (Fig. 4B). Even under strong constraints, however, payoff bias is not fully abandoned because the expected payoffs of the two strategies are more similar under strong constraints than under weak constraints (Methods; Supplementary Table 4).

Adaptive use of proximal social learning: experimental evidence

To test whether people indeed adjust their social learning strategies to the constraints of the learning environment, we ran two incentivized, preregistered experiments (Exp. 1: N = 325; Exp. 2: N = 301). Participants were tasked to acquire skills in a virtual "guild" by repeatedly choosing one of three demonstrators to learn from across 16 rounds. One demonstrator displayed high payoffs (cf. payoff bias), one had only a slightly larger skill repertoire than the participant (cf. proximal social learning), and one served as a control (Methods). By observing the outcomes of their choices, participants could learn whether payoff or proximity was the more useful cue for learning.

Participants were randomly assigned to either an unconstrained condition, in which all traits could be learned independently, or a constrained condition, in which learning success P decreased with the skill gap k between participant and demonstrator (Exp. 1: P = 1/k; Exp. 2: P = 1/k²; Methods). In a fully constrained trait hierarchy, only one of the k traits possessed by the demonstrator but not the learner is immediately learnable, giving a success probability of 1/k if each trait is equally likely to be demonstrated. Experiment 2 uses a steeper decline (1/k 2 ), consistent with demonstrators preferentially expressing more advanced traits. Accordingly, the unconstrained conditions favoured payoff bias, whereas the constrained conditions favoured proximal social learning.

These experiments isolate the core mechanism underlying our model's predictions: learning from demonstrators displaying high-payoff traits can be ineffective when differences in existing knowledge or skills constrain what can be acquired. By contrasting unconstrained learning with a simplified case of hierarchical constraints, we can test whether learners adapt their social learning strategies through experience, without the additional complexity of navigating full trait hierarchies.

Across both experiments, participants adapted their social learning strategies in line with model predictions. Experiment 1 showed that in the constrained condition, participants increasingly preferred the proximal demonstrator, and by the final rounds, selected this demonstrator far more often than in unconstrained environments (Fig. 4C, dashed lines; logistic GLMM on the last four rounds, OR = 331.92, z = 9.16, p < 0.001; Supplementary Table 5). In Experiment 2, where learning success decreased more sharply with the skill gap, the difference between conditions was even more pronounced. (Fig. 4C, solid lines; OR = 2794.29, z = 8.30, p < 0.001; Supplementary Table 5 and Supplementary Fig. 8). Conversely, both experiments showed that in unconstrained environments, participants predominantly used payoff bias, again matching the model predictions.

These findings show that people adapt their social learning strategies through experience, favouring proximal social learning when differences in knowledge or skills constrain what can be learned. Together, our models, simulations, and experiments identify a mechanism through which trait hierarchies can shape social learning, potentially explaining variation in learning strategies across real-world domains.

Discussion

Our findings show that trait hierarchy fundamentally shapes which social learning strategies are effective. Payoff bias works well when traits can be learned independently, but in domains with hierarchical trait structures, it directs learners toward advanced traits they cannot yet acquire. In such domains, individuals learn most effectively from slightly more knowledgeable peers, a strategy we formalize as proximal social learning. Our simulations and experiments further demonstrate that individuals can adaptively adopt this strategy, aligning their social learning with the constraints imposed by structured trait domains.

Our results challenge the classic prediction that copying the most successful or prestigious individuals reliably promotes adaptive learning 43 . When knowledge or skills are hierarchically organized, such strategies lead learners to attempt traits they cannot yet acquire. This helps explain why, in empirical studies, payoff bias is observed less than predicted by models 38, 52 . Our results suggest that the step-by-step acquisition of hierarchically organized traits is often more effectively guided by proximal social learning, or even arguably simpler strategies like conformity. Payoff bias may still play a role in combination with these strategies, for example by guiding learners towards valuable traits among accessible learning opportunities, or alternatively operate more indirectly by helping learners identify desirable long-term goals 93, 94 . Attention to prestigious individuals 43 may therefore reflect motives other than efficient trait acquisition, such as affiliation, social signalling, or a trade-off in which the high search costs of finding suitably advanced demonstrators make occasional failed attempts acceptable.

If social learning strategies evolve through natural selection or reinforcement learning, they should reflect the trait hierarchy of the domains in which individuals learn. Understanding human social learning, therefore, requires characterizing the kinds of trait hierarchies people encounter and how flexibly they adjust their strategies across them. Our model generates testable predictions about how social learning should vary across such domains: payoffbiased learning should prevail when dependencies are weak, whereas proximal social learning and conformity should dominate when traits are strongly hierarchical. A key next step will be to test these predictions by measuring prerequisite relationships and learning behaviour within the same real-world domains, extending the illustrative applications in Fig. 3.

The same logic extends to cultural institutions that organize trait transmission: domains with strongly hierarchical trait structures should favour scaffolded transmission systems such as apprenticeships and near-peer learning 67, 74, 76, 95, 96 because effective learning requires that learners are guided towards traits they are ready to acquire and build upon. By contrast, domains with weaker trait dependencies should permit less structured forms of transmission. Future research can test these predictions by linking variation in trait hierarchies across domains to the prevalence and effectiveness of different learning institutions.

Our results point to new directions for cultural evolution research by highlighting how trait hierarchies shape the dynamics of social learning and trait transmission. When high-payoff traits depend on low-payoff prerequisites, payoff-biased social learning may fail to build the sequences required to reach them, preventing populations from attaining globally optimal traits. By contrast, proximal social learning supports stepwise acquisition but may favour incremental, locally guided trajectories, potentially promoting more conservative patterns of cultural change. Individual differences in motivation, ability, or prior knowledge may further modulate which traits are learnable at a given stage, producing heterogeneous learning trajectories. Over longer time scales, innovations can restructure or bypass dependency relations, for example through writing systems, pocket calculators, or generative AI, thereby rewiring the pathways through which traits are built 97 . By explicitly modeling trait hierarchies and their impact on social learning strategies, our framework opens the way for studying how the structure of learning domains shapes the dynamics of cultural evolution.

demonstrators based on their knowledge difference from the learner (Δ), favouring those who are slightly more knowledgeable; one of their traits is then randomly selected.

In all cases, weighting is governed by a bias parameter b, with positive values increasing the strength of the corresponding preference. As a benchmark, we include random (unbiased) social learning, in which traits are targeted for acquisition proportionally to their frequency. For details, see section 2.3.

1.4 Analysis.

For our baseline analysis (Fig. 1), we consider all 2,045 possible acyclic networks with eight traits. This size allows for an exhaustive analysis of all possible networks (Fig. 1, 2a, d-l; we present results for smaller networks in Supplementary Fig. 10); computational demands make an exhaustive analysis of larger networks infeasible. For larger networks, we sample 50 networks at different levels of constraints (R), and use numerical simulations (Fig. 2a-c; see section 1.5). Simulation results closely match those from the analytical approach (Supplementary Fig. 11).

To characterize learning dynamics, we represent each possible repertoire as a state in a Markov chain and define transition probabilities between states based on the network structure and the learner's social learning strategy. Learning proceeds until the absorbing state in which all traits are acquired (see Section 2.4 for details). Transition probabilities depend on the traits and repertoires learners observe. To ensure tractability and comparability across strategies, we assume learners sample from a fixed "background" population, held constant across strategies. This allows us to compare strategies under identical social information, independently of how their use might alter trait frequencies over time. This background distribution is generated under random social learning and includes a small fraction (5%) of fully knowledgeable individuals to ensure that all traits are, in principle, observable. Because of hierarchical dependencies, this setup implies that more basic traits are generally more common than advanced ones.

In the baseline model, we make several simplifying assumptions. (i) Trait payoffs are independent of position in the hierarchy. (ii) All traits are equally observable, regardless of how advanced or rewarding they are; still, because traits can depend on each other, basic traits tend to be more common than advanced ones (for example, most people can add and subtract, but fewer master linear algebra). Individuals express all traits in their repertoire with equal likelihood, which further reduces the observability of advanced traits that depend on many prerequisites. (iii) Dependencies between traits are deterministic, such that learners must have all prerequisite traits before they can acquire a trait that depends on these prerequisites. (iv) Learners do not know the structure of the hierarchy, reflecting the idea that people often cannot tell whether they are ready to learn something 1, 2 . For instance, trying to learn calculus without solid algebra skills often leads to difficulties with limits, derivatives, and integrals, yet the need to master algebra first is not always obvious 3 . We relax these assumptions in subsequent analyses (Fig. 2d-l).

We evaluate performance over a limited learning horizon, where individuals benefit from acquiring valuable traits efficiently within the time available. Specifically, we measure expected payoff after five learning steps in networks with eight traits. For larger networks, we scale the learning horizon proportional to network size, using t = ⌊5/8•n⌋ (where n is the number of traits). Expected payoff is divided by t to express performance as average payoff gain per time step. Because traits have a mean payoff of 1, a value of 1 indicates that a learner is acquiring traits at exactly the average rate. Strategies perform better when they acquire high-payoff traits early and avoid failed learning attempts. Results are averaged across payoff assignments and network structures and summarized by R. The relative ranking of strategies is largely stable across different learning horizons, although performance differences diminish as learners approach complete acquisition (Supplementary Fig. 2). Analyses based on the expected time required to acquire all traits (absorption time) yield qualitatively similar results (Supplementary Fig. 6), showing that our conclusions are robust to the choice of performance measure. For details, see Section 2.4.1.5 Simulations. The simulations shown in Fig. 4 involve populations of n=1,000 individuals who build their repertoires. Individuals can learn traits organized in 100 different networks of 8 nodes each. The degree of hierarchy of these networks is characterized by either low (R=0) or high (R=1) constraints. Simulation conditions vary with respect to the frequency of networks with high constraints (denoted p). For example, if p=0.8, there are 80 networks with R=1, and 20 networks with R=0. As in the baseline model, the hierarchical structure of each learning domain is unknown to individuals.

In the main text, we focus on payoff bias and proximal social learning only. Individuals initially choose either strategy with a 50-50 probability to attempt to learn a trait. Upon experiencing the outcome of their learning attempt, they observe their current payoffs (i.e., the payoff of the selected trait if learning was successful, 0 if unsuccessful), and they update their expectations for the implemented social learning strategy through reinforcement learning. Strategies are chosen using softmax over their expected payoffs. Over time we track individuals' use of payoff bias and proximal social learning, dependent on the experienced environments (characterized by p; Fig. 4). Supplementary Fig. 9 shows results with all learning strategies. Implementation details are given in section 2.6.

1.6 Behavioural experiment.

We conducted decision-making experiments to test whether participants adjust their social learning strategies to environmental constraints. In each of 16 rounds, participants chose one of three demonstrators from whom to learn a trait: a highpayoff demonstrator (payoff bias), a slightly more knowledgeable demonstrator (proximal social learning), or a control demonstrator.

Participants were randomly assigned to either an unconstrained condition, in which all traits were learnable, or a constrained condition, in which learning success depended on the gap between the participant's and demonstrator's knowledge. These conditions respectively favoured payoff bias and proximal social learning. We conducted two preregistered experiments (n = 325 and 301) that differed in the strength of these constraints. Full details are provided in Section 2.9.

2. Implementation details

>Implementation details

2.1 Graph structure

2.1.1 DAG representation.

We represent trait hierarchies as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) 𝐺 = (𝑉, 𝐸), where nodes 𝑉 correspond to traits and directed edges 𝐸 indicate prerequisite relationships (see Figs. 1, 3, and Supplementary Fig. 1 for examples). For an edge (𝑖, 𝑗) ∈ 𝐸, trait 𝑖 is prerequisite (parent) of trait 𝑗. The ancestor set of trait 𝑗 includes all nodes reachable by following edges backward. Each graph includes a root node (trait 0) representing a naïve state with no traits. This node is always present but unexpressed and serves to anchor the dependency structure.

2.1.2 Generation of DAGs.

For the main analyses, we generate all acyclic trait hierarchies (graphs) with eight traits. Acyclicity ensures that no trait depends (directly or indirectly) on itself, which would render some traits unlearnable. We apply two steps to retain only unique dependency structures. First, we perform a transitivity reduction on all graphs to remove redundant edges. For example, if A → B and B→C, then a direct edge A→C is redundant and removed. This yields the minimal set of edges required to represent all prerequisite relations. Second, we remove isomorphic graphs using the nauty algorithm 98 . Two graphs are isomorphic if one can be transformed into the other by only relabelling the nodes. Removing such duplicates ensures that each graph in our analysis represents a distinct trait hierarchy.

2.1.3 Measure of learning constraints (R).

We quantify the strength of learning constraints using: where 𝑝̅ is the mean number of prerequisites per trait (excluding the node) and n is the number of traits.

With this normalization, 𝑅 = 0 corresponds to unconstrained learning (no prerequisites), and 𝑅 = 1 to maximally constrained learning (maximal prerequisites), where each trait depends on all preceding traits. This provides a size-independent measure of how strongly the trait hierarchy restricts learning.

2.1.4 Graph sampling.

For graphs with more than eight traits (cf. Fig. 2b-c) computational demands make an exhaustive analysis of all possible graphs infeasible. We therefore sample graphs to span the full range of constraint values. Specifically, we define 50 evenly spaced target values of 𝑅 ∈ [0,1] and generate one graph per target value.

2.2 Trait payoffs

Each trait has a payoff reflecting its contribution to performance. Payoffs vary across traits and are assigned from a set of 𝑛 -1 values (excluding the root), defined as:

This yields equally spaced values with mean 1. Because the arrangement of payoffs across the graph can strongly affect strategy performance (especially for payoff bias), we make no assumptions about which traits are more or less rewarding. Instead, we treat all assignments of payoff values to traits as equally plausible. For each graph, we therefore evaluate all (𝑛 -1)! permutations of the payoff set (with the root payoff fixed at 0) and average outcomes across these assignments. This ensures that, in expectation, no trait position is systematically associated with higher or lower payoffs. For structures with more than eight traits (cf. Fig. 2b-c), evaluating all permutations is computationally infeasible. In these cases, we approximate this average by running the model on 5,000 unique assignments per structure, sampled at random from the full set of permutations of the payoff set.

2.3 Social learning strategies

2.3.1 Overview.

In each time step, learners sample demonstrators from the population and consider traits they do not yet possess. Observed traits are weighted according to the learner's social learning strategy, and one trait is selected for a learning attempt.

Trait weights depend on both their observed frequency and the bias imposed by the social learning strategy. For strategies that operate at the level of demonstrators (prestige bias and proximal social learning), trait weights are derived from the frequencies of demonstrator repertoires that contain the trait, adjusted by the probability that the trait is expressed (see Section 2.4.3).

2.3.2 General form.

The weight assigned to trait i is: 𝑊 𝑖 = 𝑓 𝑖 • 𝑏𝑖𝑎𝑠 𝑖 Where 𝑓 is the observed frequency of the trait. The probability of attempting to learn trait 𝑖 is proportional to its weight 𝑊 𝑖 , normalized across traits:

2.3.3 Bias strength.

A parameter 𝑏 controls the strength of each bias, with higher values increasing the strength of the corresponding preference. Results in the main text use 𝑏 = 2 for all strategies (see Supplementary Fig. 4 for robustness).

where 𝐷 𝑖 is the set of demonstrator repertoires that contain trait 𝑖, 𝑓 𝑗 is the frequency of repertoire 𝑗 in the population, |𝑗| is the number of traits in repertoire 𝑗, and 𝑝𝑎𝑦𝑜𝑓𝑓 𝑗 is its total payoff. As a robustness check, we also define prestige bias based on repertoire size instead of payoff (Supplementary Fig. 5).

-Proximal social learning: traits are weighted based on how much more advanced demonstrators are compared to the learner: where ∆ 𝑗 is the number of traits in the demonstrator's repertoire that the learner does not possess.

Learners identify demonstrators with slightly larger skill sets and randomly select from the traits those demonstrators possess that the learner does not yet know.

2.4 Markov Chain

For hierarchies with up to eight traits, we analyse learning dynamics using a Markov chain over all possible learner repertoires. Each state represents a repertoire, and transitions correspond to attempts to acquire one additional trait.

2.4.1 Transition matrix.

We construct the transition matrix by determining, for each repertoire, which traits are currently learnable given the trait hierarchy. Attempt probabilities implied by the social learning strategy are assigned to these learnable traits. Probability mass assigned to non-learnable traits contributes to remaining in the same state. Repeating this procedure for all repertoire states yields the full transition matrix.

2.4.2 Outcome measures.

The Markov chain is initialized with 100% probability on the naive state (only the root trait). We compute expected payoffs over time using standard methods for finite-state Markov chains (Otto & Day, 2007). Performance is defined as the expected payoff at time t, calculated by weighting the payoff of each repertoire state by its probability under the current state distribution.

In Figs. 1, 2, and 4, we report expected payoff at time 𝑡 = ⌊ 5 8 • 𝑛⌋, where 𝑛 is the number of traits in the graph. To allow comparison across graphs of different sizes, expected payoff is divided by 𝑡. Because the average payoff of all traits is 1, a performance value of 1 therefore indicates average payoff gain per time step. Supplementary Fig. 2 shows expected payoffs for t=1-10.

To measure the expected time until all traits in the structure are known, we compute the expected time to absorption from the initial state using the transient-state submatrix and fundamental matrix of the chain 99 . Results of the absorption time match those based on expected payoffs.

2.4.3 Trait and repertoire frequencies.

Learners sample demonstrators from a fixed background population. In the baseline model, demonstrators express each trait in their repertoire with equal probability (for sensitivity tests, see Fig. 2d-e). Hierarchical dependencies shape observed trait frequencies in two ways. First, basic traits appear in more repertoires and are therefore more common. Second, traits in larger repertoires are expressed less frequently, as expression probability is distributed across more traits. As a result, advanced traits are less likely to be observed both because they occur in fewer repertoires and because they are embedded in larger ones.

We approximate these background frequencies using a two-step procedure based on random social learning. We first compute a preliminary transition matrix assuming equal trait observability, and use the resulting quasi-stationary distribution to estimate repertoire frequencies. We then derive trait frequencies from this distribution for the final analysis. To ensure that all traits remain observable, we additionally assume that 5% of the background population has acquired all traits.

2.5 Agent-based approximation of the Markov chain

For graphs with more than eight traits (Fig. 2b-c; Fig. 3), the number of possible repertoire states becomes too large for exhaustive Markov chain analysis. We therefore approximate learning dynamics using an agent-based model. We first estimate background trait frequencies via a Monte Carlo simulation. A population of 10,000 agents repeatedly attempts to learn random traits through asocial learning. A small "reset rate" (probability of replacement by a naive agent) is tuned to yield a steady-state population in which 5% of agents have acquired all traits, matching the Markov chain setup.

Using these background frequencies, we then simulate 1,000 noninteracting agents with fixed social learning strategies. At each time step, agents assign weights to unlearned traits based on their strategy and the background frequencies, and attempt to acquire one trait, as described for the Markov chain. Performance is measured as the average payoff across agents at time t. This approximation closely matches results from the Markov chain (Supplementary Fig. 11).

2.6 Agent-based model of social learning strategy preference

To study how learners adapt their social learning strategies (Fig. 4), we extend the agentbased model to allow strategy updating via reinforcement learning. We simulate 1,000 agents learning across 100 domains, each represented by a trait network that is either fully constrained or unconstrained (R=0 or R=1; cf. Fig. 1). The proportion p of constrained domains determines the overall learning environment. Initial trait distributions are generated as in Section 2.5.

Each agent maintains expected values 𝑄(𝑠) for each social learning strategy 𝑠, initialized to 1. At each time step, a random agent and domain are selected. The agent chooses a strategy using softmax selection (𝜏 = 0.05), samples 10 demonstrators, and attempts to learn a trait. The resulting payoff 𝑟 (trait payoff if successful, 0 otherwise) is used to update the chosen strategy via reinforcement learning:

𝑄(𝑠) ← 𝑄(𝑠) + 𝛼(𝑟 -𝑄(𝑠))

With learning rate 𝛼 = 0.05.

Agents have finite lifetimes (Poisson distributed with mean 0.5 ⋅ 𝑛 ⋅ 𝑑, where 𝑛 is the number of traits per domain, and 𝑑 is the number of domains), after which they are reset to the naïve state and their Q-values reinitialized. This maintains turnover and prevents unbounded accumulation of experience. We track how strategy use varies with the prevalence of constrained domains (Fig. 4; Supplementary Fig. 9 shows all strategies).

2.7 Sensitivity tests

To assess robustness, we systematically vary key model assumptions while holding others constant. Unless noted otherwise, all analyses use networks with eight traits.

2.7.1 Trait expression patterns.

In the baseline model, all traits in a demonstrator's repertoire are equally likely to be expressed. We also consider biased expression, where some traits are more likely to be observed. When a demonstrator with repertoire 𝑗 is sampled, trait 𝑖 is expressed with probability: where 𝑥 𝑖 reflects the relevant property of trait 𝑖 driving expression bias (e.g., distance from the root, or payoff) for trait 𝑖 and 𝛽 controls its strength (𝛽 = 0 recovers uniform expression; 𝛽 = 2 for Fig. 2d-e).

For preferential expression of more advanced traits (Fig. 2e), we set 𝑥 𝑖 to trait 𝑖's distance from the root node. For preferential expression of high-payoff traits (Fig. 2e), 𝑥 𝑖 equals each trait's payoff value.

2.7.2 Correlated payoffs and network structure.

In the baseline model, payoffs are independent of network position (see 2.2). We tested scenarios where more advanced traits systematically receive higher payoffs (Fig. 2f), ordering traits by their distance from the root, and assigning payoffs (generated as described in 2.2) in ascending order, so that traits requiring more prerequisites receive higher rewards.

2.7.3 Probabilistic dependencies.

The baseline model assumes deterministic dependencies: all prerequisites must be satisfied to learn a trait. We relax this assumption by allowing dependencies to be probabilistic. Each prerequisite j of trait 𝑖 has weight 𝑟 𝑗𝑖 , and the probability that trait 𝑖 is learnable is

Lower edge weights weaken dependencies, allowing traits to be acquired without all prerequisites; deterministic dependencies are recovered when 𝑟 𝑗𝑖 = 1.

For Fig. 2g-i, we vary a common edge weight from 1 to 0. Because probabilistic dependencies break transitivity, we consider two boundary cases. In the minimal edge set, only direct prerequisites are retained, so missing foundational traits affect learning indirectly. In the maximal edge set (transitive closure; used in Fig. 2g-i), each trait is directly linked to all its ancestors, so missing prerequisites directly reduce learnability. Both cases yield similar qualitative patterns, though differences between strategies are attenuated in the minimal edge set (Supplementary Fig. 12).

2.7.4 Perceived learnability.

In the baseline model, learners do not know the dependency structure. We relax this by introducing perceived learnability 𝐿 𝑖 based on the number of missing prerequisites 𝑀 𝑖 and a transparency parameter 𝜃:

As 𝜃 → 0, all traits appear equally learnable; larger 𝜃 increases discrimination against traits with many missing prerequisites. Trait weights are then given by 𝑊 𝑖 ⋅ 𝐿 𝑖 . We use 𝜃 = 1, 10, and 100 (Fig. 2j-l).

2.8 Inference of real-world trait hierarchies

We constructed plausible representations of trait hierarchies for four domains spanning diverse types of skill, and patterns of prerequisite relationships. Our aim was to examine how the model performs on empirically informed skill structures, rather than to establish the precise prerequisite relationships within each domain Because these domains differ in how knowledge is documented and transmitted, we draw on different data sources and inference methods for each: ethnographic texts, occupational co-occurrence data, and educational assessment records. We describe the inference processes below.

2.8.1 Tracking and navigation.

We inferred trait hierarchies for two learning domains using a large language model (Gemini 3 Pro Preview 100 ) applied to canonical ethnographic texts.

For tracking, we analysed The Art of Tracking by Louis Liebenberg 85 ; for navigation, We, the Navigators by David Lewis 86 . These sources describe the perceptual, cognitive, and behavioural skills underlying tracking and traditional wayfinding.

We extracted trait hierarchies using a two-stage pipeline. First, the model proposed a set of discrete, learnable skills from the source text. For each skill, it generated retrieval queries used to identify relevant passages via dense vector retrieval (bge-large-en-v1.5 101 ) with reciprocal rank fusion 102 . Skills were retained only if supported by sufficient textual evidence. See Supplementary Tables 6 and 7 for a list of all skills and their definitions.

Second, we inferred prerequisite relationships between all pairs of skills. For each pair, we retrieved passages associated with each skill and their co-occurrence and asked the model to classify their relationship (required, helpful, independent, or insufficient evidence). Only relationships classified as required prerequisites were retained as edges in the resulting networks. Classifications were based on both the retrieved textual evidence and reasoning about the component skills and their potential dependencies Prompts are provided in the SI.

2.8.2 Telemarketing.

We derived the telemarketer trait hierarchy following Hosseinioun et al. 69 using O*NET occupational skill data 103 . An occupation-skill matrix based on skill level ratings was converted into a binary network using the disparity filter 104 . Potential directional dependencies between skills were then inferred from asymmetric co-occurrence patterns across occupations. Skill pairs were retained only if their co-occurrence exceeded a null expectation, and direction was assigned based on conditional probability asymmetries. The telemarketing hierarchy was obtained by subsetting this network to skills associated with the telemarketer occupation.

2.8.3 Mathematics.

For mathematics, we use the prerequisite adjacency matrix reported for the Junyi Academy dataset by Mai et al. 87 . This network is estimated using a causal graph learning framework embedded in a differentiable Bayesian network and shows high agreement with expert annotations (90.1%). We retain edges with weight ≥ 0.7 and recode them as binary, yielding the final trait hierarchy.

2.9 Decision-making experiment

2.9.1 Overview.

Participants completed a 16-trial social learning task in which they repeatedly chose one of three demonstrators: a high-payoff demonstrator, a proximal (slightly more knowledgeable) demonstrator, or a control. Participants were randomly assigned to an unconstrained condition (all learning attempts succeed) or a constrained condition in which success declined with the skill gap between learner and demonstrator. The two preregistered experiments differed in the steepness of this constraint.

2.9.2 Sample.

Participants were recruited via Prolific (U.S.-based) and provided informed consent. Procedures were approved by the Psychology Ethics Review Board at the

1. Overview of model and experimental setup

This section provides a high-level overview of the model, analyses, and experiments; detailed implementation is described in Section 2.

1.1 Model setup.

Individuals build their trait repertoires by attempting to learn traits one by one. Traits are organized in a network of prerequisite relationships that defines their hierarchical structure. In each time step, a learner observes others ("demonstrators") and applies a social learning strategy to select a trait to attempt to acquire. Although real-world learning also involves asocial trial-and-error, practice, and refinement, we focus on social learning to isolate how different strategies cope with constraints imposed by trait hierarchies.

Learners only focus on traits they do not yet possess. In the baseline model, a learning attempt succeeds when the learner has all prerequisite traits; otherwise, it fails. Successful learning adds the selected trait to the learner's repertoire. Payoffs vary between traits, but are all positive, so individuals benefit from successful learning attempts, and benefit most by acquiring traits with high payoffs. When social learning strategies are fixed (Figs. 1 2 3), we express performance as the total payoff gain per time step; when individuals can adaptively adjust their social learning strategies with experience (Fig. 4), we express performance as the frequency at which individuals use them.

1.2 Trait hierarchies as networks.

We represent trait hierarchies as directed networks in which nodes correspond to traits and edges indicate prerequisite relationships (Fig. 1; see Supplementary Fig. 1 for additional examples). In the baseline model, a trait can be acquired only if all its prerequisite traits have been learned, imposing constraints on the order of acquisition. We quantify the strength of these constraints as the mean number of prerequisites per trait, normalized by the theoretical maximum for a network of this size (R; which explains a large portion of the variance in performance of social learning strategies, see Supplementary Table 1). Networks include a root node representing a naive state with no traits. Trait payoffs are drawn from a uniform distribution between 0 and 2, with evenly spaced values and an average payoff of 1. For details see sections 2.1-2.2.

1.3 Social learning strategies.

Individuals build their repertoires by sampling demonstrators from the population and selecting one trait to attempt to learn. Each social learning strategy assigns weights to observed traits, either directly based on their characteristics (e.g., payoff or frequency) or indirectly via the characteristics of the demonstrators who express them (e.g., total payoff or repertoire size). Individuals then select a trait with a probability proportional to these weights, ignoring traits already in their repertoire.

We consider four strategies. Payoff bias assigns weights to traits based on their payoff, favouring higher-payoff traits. Prestige bias assigns weights to demonstrators based on their total payoff (or, in a robustness check, their repertoire size; Supplementary Fig. 5), after which one of their traits is randomly selected. Conformity assigns weights based on trait frequency, favouring common traits. Proximal social learning assigns weights to University of Amsterdam (case FMG-12712). Both experiments were preregistered (Exp. 1: https://aspredicted.org/hy5i3g.pdf; Exp. 2: https://aspredicted.org/8sp2np.pdf).

Sample sizes were determined via a priori power analysis (target 𝑁 = 300 per experiment; 80% power to detect log-odds ≈ 0.42 under conservative assumptions; see preregistrations for details). We excluded participants who failed attention checks; oversampled participants who passed were retained. In Experiment 1, 325 participants were included (low constraints: n=171; high constraints: n=154) and 116 excluded (n=58; n=58). In Experiment 2, 301 participants were included (n=151; n=150) and 6 excluded (n=1; n=5; see Section 2.9.5).

2.9.3 Task and design.

The task was framed as a guild apprenticeship. On each trial, participants observed three demonstrators characterized by (i) payoff (coins earned) and (ii) skill gap relative to the participant. Demonstrators were constructed such that one maximized payoff, one minimized skill gap, and one served as a control, creating trade-offs between these cues (Supplementary Table 3; Screenshot 6). Payoff ranges differed across experiments: Experiment 1 used evenly spaced bands, whereas Experiment 2 shifted these to match the expected returns of payoff-based and proximal strategies across conditions (Supplementary Table 4).

Participants received a base payment of £1.50 plus a performance-based bonus determined by a randomly selected trial (conversion: £0.01 per coin; maximum possible bonus: £1.00). The task took approximately 6 minutes on average. Mean bonuses were £0.42 (Exp. 1) and £0.52 (Exp. 2), corresponding to mean total payments of £1.92 and £2.02 (~£19-20/hour).

2.9.4. Constraint manipulation.

In the unconstrained condition, all learning attempts succeed. In the constrained condition, success depends on the skill gap 𝑘 between learner and demonstrator. In Experiment 1, success declines as: and in Experiment 2 as:

These functions reflect a fully constrained trait hierarchy (cf. Fig. 1; 𝑅 = 1) in which demonstrators 𝑘 steps ahead may express one of 𝑘 traits, only one of which is learnable. As a result, payoff bias is favored in the unconstrained condition, whereas proximal social learning is favored in the constrained condition (Supplementary Table 4).

2.9.5 Attention checks.

Participants completed attention checks during the task. Experiment 1 included a single check; Experiment 2 used two more explicit checks to improve compliance (Screenshots 9 and 10). Participants who failed were excluded from analysis.

2.9.6 Preregistered and reported analyses.

The preregistered confirmatory analysis focused on the final four rounds (13-16), where participants were expected to have settled on a strategy. Choices were coded as proximal (1) versus payoff (0). We estimated mixed-effects logistic regression models with constraint condition as predictor and random intercepts for participants.

Preregistered hypotheses predicted greater payoff-based choice in the unconstrained condition and greater proximal choice in the constrained condition (one-sided tests, 𝛼 = .05). Experiment 2 additionally tested (i) a direct low-versus-high condition comparison, and (ii) an across-rounds analysis testing whether proximal choices increased over rounds more strongly in the constrained condition (round × condition).

In the main text, we report the direct comparison between unconstrained and constrained conditions in the final four rounds for both experiments (Fig. 4c; Supplementary Table 5). This comparison was preregistered for Experiment 2 and is reported as an additional analysis for Experiment 1.

All panels show performance of social learning strategies relative to random social learning (dotted lines at y=1) as a function of learning constraints (R). a-c, Varying the number of traits. d-f, Varying payoff structure and trait expression (d, advanced traits expressed more often; e. high-payoff traits expressed more often; f, advanced traits have higher payoffs); g-i, Varying the strictness of trait dependencies. j-l, Allowing individuals to have knowledge about trait hierarchy ("transparency"). Absolute performance values are shown in Supplementary Fig 7; implementation details in Methods.

R denotes the normalized mean number of prerequisites per trait, reflecting the strength of learning constraints. The difference between telemarketing and mathematics, despite similar levels of constraint, is consistent with the stronger effects observed in larger domains (Fig.2A-C).
